ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u4	First of all, Mr President, I would like to ask for your tolerance on my behalf, but also on behalf of the other rapporteurs, because today the issues that we are going to discuss are very important and numerous. I would therefore like to begin with the issues relating to Article 62 and Article 86. These two provisions fall within a framework which we could call 'the protection framework for representatives of the Greek people' on the one hand 'and the members of the government' on the other. And the question straightaway arises: is there really, in favour of these people, discrimination on the part of the legislator who can support this negativeism, which has occurred over the last ten years, which has taken the form of anti-parliamentism which is expressed in a variety of ways and which is condensed in a phrase, in the so-called'representative betrayal'? This is what, once in the beginning of the last century, was called for by intellectuals, by Zillin Benta in France, today comes through this climate of reviving the very long and negative debate that is taking place against parliamentaryism to concentrate literally and to be systematically cultivated with regard to the so-called privileges of MPs and ministers. Ladies and gentlemen, we must give answers. Is this a privileged treatment? Is this really what we are all saying that the legislator gives a margin of immunity without any reason for the Members and the Ministers? We need - and you will allow me to - a historic regression. And because the best way to refer to an issue is to start with one of the myths used. And because the myths have an attractive structure, they are much more visible, but they also create much better meanings when we are going to deal with a complex issue, I will refer to the myth that he uses in his dialog "Political" Plato and refers to the passage of people from Saturn to the age of Dios. There was, in fact, a time when Saturn's time, where people lived beyond the needs and had no concern for the service of their society's personal or collective purposes. He who dealt with the service and fulfillment of all needs was the greatest demon, it was the time of paradise. At some point, however, they were forced to pass into the kingdom of Zeus, to pass into the kingdom of need, and from there on, in what Plato says "in the ocean of the unequality of human relations" to be a rhythm, to enter a class. And then, the position of the greatest demon was taken by politics and the politician became the one who deals with the management of common affairs in such a way that what Plato said at the time was satisfied that "policy is the greatest gift given to people to solve the problems among themselves, without being slaughtered." So what we are saying today is a policy and what we mean when we talk about a political system is not something that we absolutely have to disintegrate, or we have to regard it as a permanent spot in society. It is indeed the only way to resolve common affairs. That is why, from a recommendation of political art as a medicine for healing and treating social needs within the city, it has since begun what we call "responsible" at the beginning of the supreme ruler as a form of protection of the one who takes the honour of dealing with the administration of the common things. Then, precisely, this irresponsibility did not endure, because at that time there were voices saying that people cannot be distinguished from those who govern and have no responsibility, and those who have all the responsibilities. That is why we passed to the time when in the context of the democracy of Klistheni there was the institution for the rulers who were originally called'responsible'. In general, of course, it is'responsibility' and because everything is paralyzed over the centuries,'responsible' has now become'responsible'. And for her, then, we will speak pre-introductoryly, which then and when we arrived in new times began to take the form of political or parliamentary responsibility. In this respect, there was united, and criminal and political responsibility was also in this sense in the era of the crown, the pre-democratic, in England, those who took over office were obliged to be accountable to the representative bodies. However, by reaching the younger ages, criminal liability was indeed specified. And in order to reach ourselves, the first great attempt to systemize this phenomenon, namely the criminal responsibility of the rulers, is the rhetoric of Ioannis Arabantinos in 1880, known as "Acts on the responsibility of the rulers and ministers". From there the dilemma is being raised for the first time: yes, the rulers have a responsibility, yes, they must be subject to the law, but care must be taken so that this is not the way the rulers are treated on the one hand in the exercise of their powers, on the other hand on the other hand on the other hand on the means of dispute and on the means of dissent on the part of those with whom they have political differences. The first, the so-called pre-revolutionary constitutions, were in Greece to have arrangements in line with the current rules of Article 86. And, of course, this was not by chance because, as was the case then in the fight for independence, this very notion of how it is served and how the ruler is being protected against the crimes or malicious actions or proceedings against them, we saw it being in the case of Petrobe Mavromichalis, the Brothers Metaxas, the practice now, the historical form of dealing with him that led to the first Civil War, because these Ministers were accused of beginning this whole story in exactly the same way that, in the context of the division, it is essentially pervading our entire history until now, we had the same model to dominate relations between Trikoupi and Deliyannis, in relations between Gounari and Venizelos, in relations - and later in the newest, in 1989 - of the so-called conflict between PASOK and the right and so-called right. That is why, ladies and gentlemen, what we need to have in mind is that it is necessary with coolness, with clear consciousness and especially a very tight heart - there is no need for sentiment here - to decide how the issue of the criminal responsibility of the ministers and the State Secretary and the Prime Minister himself, as well as of the President of the Republic, should be dealt with from the legal order in Greece. Is this really a special treatment introduced by Article 86? No, there is no special or privileged treatment, because the law does not discriminate in its application against cases of civil and non-political crimes. There is equality of criminal law and no one is privileged. Moreover, Article 86 itself states that no special or ministerial offences apply to Greece. That once existed. There's no today. Therefore, equal treatment is fully restored in relation to criminal law and the ministers and the State Secretarys and ordinary citizens. What's the matter with him? The issue remaining after the 2001 Review, which has indeed brought very important reforms to the institutional responsibility of the ministers and the State Secretarys, is the procedural right, namely the exercise of the criminal prosecution, which has been entrusted exclusively to the House. Because there has already been the abolition of interrogation and pre-reviews by Parliament, as it existed before 2001, and the occupation has been abolished and, if you like, the exercise of public prosecutors' duties in proceedings against Ministers and State Secretarys by members of the National Representation. These aren't today. On the other hand, where the matter is limited it is in Article 86(1). But one will say: 'But why should this distinction still exist?' The reasons are very simple. Firstly, precisely because this treatment is an institutional guarantee of protection of the Ministers and of the State Secretarys, not just because they have a distinct or privileged position within the social area, but because the seriousness of their responsibility in the exercise of governmental functions makes it necessary to protect them, because this is not how government work is blocked, nor is the ministers or the Deputy Prime Ministers themselves or the Prime Minister or the Prime Minister expose himself to this prerogative which can create unnecessary or inappropriate criminal persecutions. The House would say that the exercise of all the responsibilities stemming from the people, because it is the only body that exercises popular sovereignty, can even, on behalf of the people, exercise such powers. It can, of course, and it is reasonable to say that here we have no divergence from the system of separation of powers. Of course we have, but this is excellent and individual and is justified on the basis of the principle of necessity, because there is no other instrument necessary and suitable to deal with and cure this need. I now come to see what is today the specific amendment to Article 86(3), which is essentially a contribution from all parties. This, ladies and gentlemen, is the obstacle which, until now, did not allow the House to function in a way which had to be fair and which had the right to do so, because, where Parliament had to carry out its duties, there was the short deadline for extinguishing, which imposed a ban on fleeing after the expiry of the first parliamentary session, after the period during which the specific crime is being persecuted was committed. This is now being abolished and, as a result of this barbarity, it is in time for Parliament to be able to exercise its right to prosecute the so-called'ministerial' crimes. And that, of course, because, like the science of constitutional law and the theory, and politics, it has been accepted, if there was any obstacle, was, as I said, this short depreciation deadline and, unfortunately, the so-called'majority principle' must be clearly recognised. I shall start by saying that this is primarily a matter of direct political interest and, more generally, a question of the quality of political life and the credibility of the political world and precisely of the distrust which the voters often have with regard to political order. The general rapporteur for the majority was right to refer to the need through Article 86 to regulate two conflicting constitutional principles first. One is the principle of equality. The second is a peculiar institutional guarantee designed to protect the Minister when he legally carries out his duties. So I will start from what the Communist Party of Greece is counting on us. Why do we not abolish Article 86 as a whole? The answer is simple. In all the constitutions of Europe there is a distinction between those acts that are closely linked to the exercise of the minister's function. In French, for example, the theory speaks of actes rattachables, acts that are linked and activities that are not linked to its responsibilities. For them, therefore, the acts associated with the exercise of the minister's authority and to protect him from the pressure, which can often reach the limits of the blackmailing pressure of organized interests to guide him as to how to act or prevent him from exercising his powers, is usually a filter between the charge and the referral and the criminal court. What actions is this about? Above all, it must not be possible for the Minister to give his signature in such a way as not to blame him, without taking into account all the facts of the House, and to determine whether there is indeed a case of violation on the part of the Minister, or whether the motive for the appeal is another, blackmail or avengement to be checked by the Minister. That is why the institutional guarantee only works where this association of the minister's powers with the crime. What are clear acts outside the scope of Article 86? First of all, what goes on in the field of the private life of the Minister, if he embezzles with money from his sister or if he or she or she has made a gesture against his secretary, even his secretary at the Ministry. There has been a debate and there SYRIZA's position was the complete abolition, it was not the interpretative shape. I now come to the other issues relating to Article 54 and so on. Since the first moment, as you know, the JRC has dealt with the issue of Greeks voting without small-party purposes, without regularism, without counting beans and with the aim of finding a solution that will facilitate Greeks who live, work or study abroad, who have ties with the country and who may also have their hope of returning to the country, to exercise their electoral right from the place of residence. At the same time, however, a solution must be found which, on the one hand, guarantees this right, but on the other hand prevents the electorate from being altered by voters who, perhaps, have no knowledge of the Greek political reality, have no real ties with the country and, above all, do not exist or suffer the consequences of any political choice. We are talking, in other words, about people who, because of their long stay abroad, may have been born abroad or are descendants of old immigrants, have objectively incorporated into the societies of these countries, have well or badly - it is objective - link their lives and their future to these societies and, most importantly, form a political consciousness and criterion based on developments, on the relationship and on the political reality in these countries, which may differ radically from the reality that we live in our own country. That is why the JRC's proposal had three main axes in order to exercise the electoral right at the place of residence and to be able to count on the electoral correlation. The first axis is the existence of real economic and social links with Greece, such as, for example, tax obligations and not just a mental bond, which no one disputes that there may be, but it is not enough. After all, even at the core of the urban perception of formal equality of voting rights is the correlation between the right to vote and the existence of obligations, namely, to suffer the consequences, whatever consequences, from the political choice it makes. The second axis of our proposal is the existence of a maximum, reasonable time absence from the country or vice versa of a reasonable minimum presence area in the country, mainly for documentation purposes. The third criterion was the presence in elections of the foreigners who guarantee the validity of the vote, but above all it guarantees essential political interest and participation, which must be ensured by participation in an electoral process. With these criteria, in our view, those who left in the years of the crisis and the memoranda are also covered, so that we can say that better than those who have been persecuted by the governments with their policy over the years of the crisis and the memoranda, because immigration, like refugee, is not a natural phenomenon. No one leaves to another country, just because he hobbies or tourism, leaves because there is such a need. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] Finally, in relation to the criteria, we stressed that these should be safeguarded and shielded constitutionally and applied to all those who want to join the special electoral lists abroad, without any distinction between old and new registered. Let us now see what data we have taken into account in shaping this proposal, which is clean, comprehensive, coherent and accepted. First of all, we have taken into account the fact - something that no one says - that in Greek citizenship law - contrary to what is happening in other states too - the ancestor dominates - despite the changes that have been made - the anachronistic law of blood, which results from those who have Greek origin, only one ancestor - and this ancestor may be too far away - to be able to obtain Greek citizenship. It means that they can easily obtain Greek citizenship from other countries, as long as they can prove that they have even a distant ancestor from Greece. So what the government said at first, but also other parties, and the Change Movement and the Mera25, which criticises the New Democracy, is retreating from its initial positions - that the regulation must concern all those who are registered, in our view, is half the truth. And why is half the truth? Because it is aware that under the current Greek law of citizenship, there is probably the possibility of being registered on the electoral lists, of obtaining voting rights, and therefore the right to decisive intervention in political developments, many more thousands of people who come from Greece, although they love Greece - no one disputes this - yet they have been cut off from the Greek reality and do not form a criterion based on this reality. The second presumption that we have taken into account is international experience and international practice. Most countries, particularly the European countries, the United Kingdom, Germany and the United States of America, all make conditions for the exercise of the electoral right by their own voters, who live abroad, while some other countries, for example Ireland or Israel, for example, forbid it for good reasons. These conditions are neither a restriction nor a result of the electoral right. They are ways of facilitating the exercise of the electoral right by joining the special electoral lists abroad. This proposal, in our view, is also differentiated from the original proposal from the New Democracy, PASOK or Mera25, but also from the original proposal from SYRIZA. What for? Because, in our view, SYRIZA's proposal opened a dangerous ferry. Who's that? It allows the exercise of the right to vote on the basis of unlimited blood law. Even if this is done for a limited number of Members, even if this is done without calculation in the correlation of power, it gives the right, based on the law of blood, to vote for up to 3 million people. So it's a coup d'etat. We cannot accept that there is a formal equality of vote - in fact there is no anyway - when there is an election law that keeps a motto and a bonus. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u220	Thank you, Mr. Heta. I would now ask Mrs Adamopoulou, on behalf of Mera25, to take the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u221	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, today's agenda is on top of the agenda with the revised proposals for Articles 62 and 86 of the Constitution, on parliamentary immunity and criminal liability and prosecution of ministers. These proposals, supported by a fairly reinforced majority in the previous House, with the exception of the introduction of an interpretative declaration on offences, which was made in the course of the exercise and not at the occasion of the ministerial service. This means, therefore, that this House only needs one hundred and fifty votes to identify the two provisions of the revised, which are the two sides of the same currency and which have been afflicted for years in political reality and in social dialogue. The current Parliament will therefore be called upon by the minimum predicted majority to regulate two procedures, which experience shows that they are legally suffering, are deeply affecting the institutional authority of both politics and justice. In theoretical dialectic, but also in international practice, the criminal liability of the ministers is regulated according to the standards of two main schools. One acknowledges the need for a member of the government to go through the way of the House before the threshold of justice is passed, while the other considers such preparatory procedures unnecessary, believing that the House is only entitled to seek political responsibilities, but never to be involved in the criminal proceedings. This is an approach that avoids criminalisation of political life and thus equates the member of the government with the citizen on the part of the criminal procedure. Here we must remind historically that the House as a body of criminal responsibility was initiated by the United Kingdom, because in the 17th and 18th centuries there was not yet the so-called parliamentary responsibility, that is to say, the search for political responsibilities and the search for criminal responsibilities by the parliaments covered this gap. So look at what a wonderful institutional example the British Members themselves gave. When parliamentary responsibility was established, the House has already decided in 1804 not to seek criminal responsibilities of Ministers, although it has had every legal right. The House is also not involved in France, Germany, Ireland, Spain, with the exception of certain political crimes. While in Italy the sole competence of the House is to discontinue the procedure if it considers that the Minister has acted to protect a critical constitutional public interest. Therefore, we have four major prerogatives of European legal culture, which represent both major schools of law worldwide, which adopt a system of zero or minimum intervention by parliament on matters of criminal liability of Ministers. In Greek state texts, on the other hand, special arrangements for the responsibility of ministers have been in place since 1822. We have been insisting for almost two hundred years on a system, which retains its basic architecture, which has led us to a civil strife and under which fifteen Prime Ministers have been accused, and yet the famous clearance of the political system has never occurred. If we add the courtesy of micropolitical involvement, manipulation and yellowism, we can safely talk about a fiasco. A fiasco that arrived at the Nadir when a parliament decided to prosecute former ministers even though the offences had obviously been dropped. However, neither New Democracy nor SYRIZA with their proposals seem to want to solve these problems definitively. Because they still accept the wrong assumption that political and criminal responsibility are involved in a procedural process, while it is clear that other acts and omissions, which are detrimental to the public interest and need a political valuation, should be attributed political responsibility and other criminal behaviours which should be the sole responsibility of justice. It is indeed possible - because this was also raised as a matter of concern in committee - a minister or a Member - and the practice has shown it - to deal with abuses and sometimes see political feasibility. It is not only the ministers and MPs who are vulnerable to unfair legal adventures or to pretext complaints that hide a political sign. At the same time, officials from local government may need to be subjected to local government officials, judges, prosecutors, hospital administrators, officials who carry out inspections, managers of public enterprises, a circle of people who are called to take decisions that may be sad. So the question is: Do these people who perform such important tasks do not need the corresponding institutional guarantees with that logic? Does it not conflict with the principle of equality to exclude them from a corresponding institutional protection umbrella, since they are at corresponding risk to Members and Ministers? We understand, however, that this, legally necessary, extension is one that puts the problem in its right dimension, because it shows the paradox, that we still have to seek equality within the same inequality. And I am talking about the primary blow to equality that wants ministers and MPs to have privileged treatment in criminal matters in relation to all other citizens. So that creates the problem, and that was identified by the European legal classes I mentioned and rejected. Although the predominant terminology speaks of criminalisation of political life, in Mera25 we are sure that we must put an end to the politicisation of criminal life. The Committee of Inquiry itself is absolutely sufficient, in order to carry out its control role, and to seek, in depth, policies and only responsibilities for acts or omissions of Ministers. For all the rest, regular justice must be undertaken by reason of its competence, as the principle of separation of powers and the principle of equality stipulates. In this respect, furthermore, Professor Androullakis agrees on the criminal doctrine and on his part Professor Kasimatis, who notes: "Let the current privileged criminal prosecution system of the members of the government be lifted immediately and in his position to establish that, from now on, the ministers will be persecuted and tried like all citizens, but with an absolute degree of time, so as to put an end to this political negligence that harms democracy". There are therefore other ways of satisfying the law - we would say - requesting enhanced protection, which are known to us, always through justice. We are entrusting the exercise of criminal prosecutions for criminal offences of Ministers to senior prosecutors and, as we are suggesting, to ensure that their experience and independence are the ultimate institutional guarantee, to guarantee an effective, rapid and fair crisis and so that the political system and society are not jeopardised by various delays that feed back Scandinavia. It was heard as a post on the New Democracy's side of the committee that judges could not control political feasibility. Is it not enough to simply check if the Minister committed a criminal offence? If justice finds that there is indeed a glimpse of the Minister's criminality, would it matter who denounces it and whether this is going to happen to micro-policy? And vice versa, if an independent judiciary finally considers that a lawsuit or petition is a product of unfounded claims - whoever they are - can't file the case, self-address the malicious complainant himself by judging, by the way, then that there was political feasibility? Inside this crisis, can it not include assessments, which have to do with the purpose of the treacherous messenger and take a clear position? So behind these positions, which require special privileges, there is a deep question for civil servants in justice, which is also a sign of the crisis of parliamentism and constitutionalism. Therefore, as Mr Vlachopoulou says, 'it is impossible for a constitutional provision to be based on and reliant on the institutions of justice'. Mr Venizelos also found it when he wrote "I think maintaining this House's competence in most of the modern constitutions is a manifestation of distrust over the political impartiality of the judiciary". Later, however, judging by his position, he implied that the prosecutors may be moving on the basis of some political stepping-by-step, and so they may be abusive, and we may cause a breach in relations between political and judicial authority. So he becomes a skeptic himself. However, it is not enough either for those who insist on the maintenance of the problem. Are you concerned about the openness of the courts because of their political attitude and formation, and that is why you are deciding to trust the politicians, who are already covered by the presumption of political feasibility? How well, then, Professor Chrysanogonos himself puts it! He says: "If the political impartiality of the judges is questionable or questionable, the political impartiality of the Members is by definition non-existent. Or, to put it more modestly, why should we be judged that the political impartiality of Members is undeniable?' So, for the ministers, we claim to be the same as for the citizens, and in this way we solve the dilemma 'in the exercise of or on the occasion of duties', and we say: both in the exercise and on the occasion. In this way, we simplify the issue, we resolve all the issues of expropriation deadlines and all the issues of the elimination of expulsion, which are now finally being granted immunities. Finally, together with the ban on proprietaryity, we entrust a council of three Vice-Presidents of the Ice Bay with the absolute priority of examining any complaint. Our proposal respects the distinction of powers, trusts the Greek judge, restores equality and ultimately relieves parliamentism from the deterioration of micropolitical toys and manipulations. The same point of view is taking our turn in the matter of parliamentary immunity. The MEP is at risk much less than the Minister and is already protected by Rule 61 for an opinion or vote he gave in the course of his parliamentary duties. Members of Parliament's criminal affairs have no place in Parliament's headquarters and Parliament has no place in the criminal investigation of MPs. As in the case of the Ministers, regular justice can best guarantee the protection of the parliamentary authority. And here experience confirms to us, the immunity that is supposed to protect our authority comes itself and undermines it. That is why the citizens are calling for a profound change in the rule of immunity and responsibility of Ministers. I will repeat what I said in committee: 82% say 'the politicians cannot decide whether or not to refer to justice'. We are listening to this request and we come to a simple and understandable solution: the same criminal treatment between citizens, MPs or ministers. No privilege, no distinct treatment. After all, our political morality requires that the official give a good example of equality and honesty. We are an equal part of the people and even with increased commitments. In total, we ask the Members to be subject to the regular criminal proceedings, with the exception of the acts relating to their free and uninterrupted parliamentary action, for which Parliament's permission is needed. However, the model of the pre-examination committees looking for criminal responsibilities is also logical. Such committees are totally unnecessary. When justice takes over, there is no need for a Member to act as the prosecutor. That is why we are abstaining from this hypocrisy. The question is: when did the Committees of Inquiry fall light? Have they escaped micropolitical disease, politicians and journalists from the little screen and front pages? Are the guarantees kept and the presumption of innocence? Some defend the work of the criminal committees of inquiry and consider them necessary, at the same time as we are all seeing the committee on the case of NOVARTIS working day by day, called by a Member of the junta commission, to hear that there is a disgrace in what was sacred in this House, to see a majority pressing with a heavy anti-institutional hand, resorting to unusual enforcement tactics, even to debate the guard of this House itself. No man who cares about Greece would like, therefore, to be involved in a committee which insults and discredits the country. That is why we do not legalise the committee in our presence, the reason why we will not legalise such committees in future and which we are striving to abolish. So all in justice and only in justice. In relation to Rule 54 and the simple analogue: it is well known that Mera25 would like to be open, simple analogue, without a threshold for entry into the House, and even without a Member of the State, with a people-balanced electoral districts and with an explicit abolition of the one-sided and double-sided elections, which operate in favour of bipartisanism and undermine pluralism. It is the only electoral system, which promotes genuine representation of the people's will in Parliament and which ensures, as is said, the genuine recording of social and political ties. The simple analogue actually makes everyone's vote equal and promotes healthy multilingual parliamentaryism, giving each parliamentary group the power it deserves. Thus, consents multiply, encourage the collaboration of the many and the winners end up with our political culture and public interest. And it is interesting that the government, which today supports increased self-restraints, because only five years ago, in 2014, it concluded that the analogue system is more in line with the needs of the expansion of democratic legitimacy of the Parliament, as a consequence of which the government is based on the confidence of the Parliament. Why today is the simple analogue for the government destructive for the country? So the answer is again in the neoliberal doctrine. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u226	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the 1975 Constitution, with the successive revisions that have taken place since then, gave a solid basis for the development of the post-political parliamentary democracy in Greece. Despite the problems that have existed for forty-four years, this Constitution has guaranteed the country an undisturbed democratic parliamentary life, a life that has been unprecedented for the political data of the new Greek state, as created by the struggle for liberation until today. That is why I think we should all assess it very positively. We must also say and evaluate very positively the contribution of all the political forces from 1975 to now, from the Right to the Left, to the joint creation of institutions that have endured, which have offered and placed the post-Greek state in the front line between the rest of the European states. However, after a prolonged ten-year economic crisis, which has not only affected the economy, but has profoundly affected Greek society, the country is beginning to recover financially. Of course, this recovery is accompanied by a political maturity among the citizens, who are turning back the bad practices of the past. This transformation of the Greek economy and society to meet the challenges of the 4th industrial revolution cannot achieve this without the necessary reforms and it is true that the Greek society itself is calling for these reforms to go ahead. However, this demand for society once again cannot be achieved on the basis of the existing constitutional provisions. I think that's what we all agree on. Or if not we all agree, at least most. However, some political forces - and I am referring above all to the Civic Opposition - instead of taking advantage of this social movement and this pressure from society for reform, unfortunately did not do so during the previous period of the Revision House, in my view, unequivocally. Inner-party purposes or even ideologies have led to a reality of excluding many articles from the previous review process. And that's not something that can just cause us grief. It's extremely painful. Ladies and gentlemen, you know - we have said so many times - that the constitutional review is not a frequent procedure for parliamentary practice. It is a process, which is repeated on average every ten years. We have lost an opportunity to respond to what the Greek society itself put forward as a key demand. We left behind provisions proposed by the majority today during the previous process of the House, which could, to a large extent, respond to the challenges of the times. But we are here to complete this process with what we have finally chosen to change and send a strong signal to all those who, possibly, build political careers, fuelling parliamentaryism and democracy. Today's interventions remain important, because they demonstrate that our democracy remains strong. And the people's sovereignty, which, according to our Constitution, stems from the people and is exercised through us, is exercised for all citizens without discrimination and separation, protecting their individual, social and political rights. With the amendments that we are proposing, we restore the food prestige of the political world by means of provisions that enhance the transparency of political life, promote parliamentary control, ensure unresolved political action and address corruption with determination. I think that it is precisely in this context that the proposal for a revision of Articles 62 and 86 is part of the proposal. Ladies and gentlemen, the safeguarding and functioning of the representative system, as a modern state, is based on two main pillars: the representative, who is the people, and the representative, who is the Member, who must exercise his parliamentary duties with full freedom of opinion, action and vote against conscience. This function is performed by the institution of parliamentary immunity, which is intended to guarantee the Member as an institution and not as a person, in order to enable him to perform his duties unaffected by any kind of distraction that would be intended to manipulate his/her mind. It is true that in recent years public opinion has taken a negative picture from various events in the national Parliament. I think that no one can argue with that. For several years a bad practice has been established that no parliamentary immunity is lifted for anyone and any accusations. This picture is wrong and we must have the courage to admit it. And it is important that at practical level in recent years this picture has changed sharply. And, above all, it changed with the contribution of those colleagues who, even in cases where they could ask for and enjoy immunity, asked the House to waive immunity, for this benefit to be waived. But on the other hand, I cannot stand by the bad practice of Members of today's Representation during the previous parliamentary term, when on the pretext of the ministerial status of MPs they provoked the lifting of their immunity, in fact guaranteeing impunity. So they did not protect Parliament's authority. On the contrary, they offered an alibi to all those forces of populism, who are trying to identify the institution with a synergyal attitude, and as a result once again the authority of the political world to accept a strong blow. However, we cannot deny the reality that the exercise of parliamentary duties takes place in a conflictual social and political environment. In these circumstances, the Member is exposed to all kinds of pressures to act favorably or hostilely in one or the other direction. The purely political and functional nature of the parliamentary immunity and of the Member's impunity is therefore obvious. This is not a personal privilege for the Member, but an institutional guarantee of the smooth functioning of parliamentaryism and the democratic principle. That is why it is necessary to further ensure that the Member is assured, by introducing a provision which must be geared towards the extremism of the political wretched by the persecution of MPs. At the same time, however, it must also discourage the circumvention of the institution. In this context, I believe that the proposal for the revision of Article 62, tabled by the New Democracy, is being initiated. More specifically, we are proposing the strictening of the institution of immunity referred to in Article 62, with a provision designed not to undermine the impunity of Members and to offer immunity as a shield to avoid conceived of their judicial prosecution. On the contrary, we shield the parliamentary and political action and expression of the Member from malicious persecution. We are also doing this by expressing our support for the Constitution that no immunity can be provided with what is not related to it, as Parliament, otherwise, is obliged to waive immunity. We believe that the spirit with which the New Democracy proposes the revision of Article 62 responds to the Greek people's request, but in fact also to the experience gained by Members and colleagues in the Greek Parliament. For Article 86 now. The second revised provision, which relates to the honour of the political world and its prestige, is that of Article 86. The great question of modern representative democracy is to defend its prestige, credibility and capacity for contact with society and the people, namely, essentially to defend politics. The main demand, therefore, is that we stop surrounding the country's political world with a allegedly dark presumption of guilt for a alleged violation of corruption behaviour. Civilian staff and democratic citizens must be protected against communication, I would say, a system which is often defamatory, equitable, unfair and does not respect the rules of the rule of law, rules which must, above all, apply to the criminal proceedings, but, in particular, to the criminal proceedings. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u276	Thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, it is a great honour to me that today's suggestion to Parliament in a top institutional function of the state, the constitutional revision. Today we are discussing the thematic section referred to in Articles 54 and 56 concerning the vote of Greek voters abroad, the electoral system and the blockades of Members' election. The provisions of Articles 54 and 56 were considered revised in the previous House by a marginal majority. But let us begin, with the sensitive issue, the national issue of the vote of Greek voters abroad, who have been very concerned about public debate lately. It was a pre-election commitment by Prime Minister Kyriakos Mitsotakis, the possibility for Greek voters who live abroad, to finally be able to vote from their place of residence in the next elections. As we all understand, this is a self-evident obligation for the Greek state to our compatriots, who live and work outside Greece, to facilitate the exercise of their electoral right, as is the case with the vast majority of the countries of the Council of Europe, who have fulfilled this self-evident obligation to their citizens. Of the forty-seven countries only seven have not solved this issue to date and one of them, unfortunately, is Greece. It is an obligation, although it had been legally foreseen both in the 1975 Constitution, and we are talking about almost half a century ago - as well as in the 2001 Constitution, it has not so far been implemented by our political system. So basically we are talking about our brothers, who because of the recent economic crisis have suffered an informal, but real persecution from their country, and who, not only cannot return, but are not even involved in shaping the election result. Our resignation has repeatedly submitted bills, as well as proposals for legislation to introduce the vote of Greek voters abroad and, indeed, already since 2009 on the Minister of Prokopi Pavlopoulou as Minister of Foreign Affairs. However, the relevant bill that was tabled at the time was not voted for by the necessary number of permanent MPs, by any of the opposition officers. Subsequently, both in 2016 and 2017, our prolongation has tabled proposals for legislation, but they have not been accepted by the previous parliamentary majority. Finally, we also tabled a specific proposal in the previous House for the revision of Article 51 of the Constitution to allow Greeks who live abroad only for party or party coalition and to be elected MPs representing Greek foreignism to vote. However, this proposal was also rejected, unfortunately, by the previous parliamentary majority and therefore the provision of Article 51 was not considered to be a reviewer. But it is now time for Greek foreign voters to exercise this self-evident right and to vote for the first time at their place of residence. For this reason, the Prime Minister has opened intensive consultations with all the political leaders, who have come to a draft law, which we seek to vote on if possible, not only by the two parliamentarians, as the Constitution defines, but also unanimously by the three hundred, thus putting our party purposes aside, for the benefit of all Greek citizens. In the intergroup committee set up to this end, a four-axle basis has been established. The first axis is the equal vote of the Greeks who live abroad with the voters inside. In other words, the vote of the Greeks abroad is also counted on the final election result, like all other Greek citizens. The second axis is Greek foreigners voting for the party's vote of State that they wish to vote on. Furthermore, to increase the number of MPs from twelve to fifteen, so that the parties, if they so wish, can place themselves in election posts from abroad. The third axis is the vote to take place in the local embassies and consulates of Greece or but certain voting shops, where the self-representation of Greeks abroad will be required at the points where the polls will be based. Here, of course, I would like to point out - the obvious thing about us - that the New Democracy's position has been and remains the possibility of broad use of the letter of vote, in order to facilitate voters who live in remote areas. Sadly, though, because some have disagreed, and in order to find a well-considered solution to this issue, the Prime Minister retreated in his proposal to use a letter of vote, ending in the solution to the voting process only at consulates or designated stores. Finally, the fourth axis concerns the thorny subject of the number of foreign voters on the special electoral lists. There it was agreed that those Greeks would be able to vote for less than thirty-five years, provided that within this period there are only two years left in Greece. Just then, within the framework of this intergroup agreement and on the basis of the relevant governmental commitment, our proposal, before you, has been tabled for the revision of Article 54, with the addition of paragraph 4. On this proposal I would like to make a few points. It is clear that this paragraph would be more appropriate to add to Article 51 of the Constitution. However, as I said, unfortunately, this was not considered revised by the previous House, and so it would not be possible to add it now. The proposed amendment is also made as a more specific and therefore a predominant provision, including in the corpus of the Constitution the specific conditions for facilitating the exercise of that right, i.e. not restrictive. This is done in order to avoid any possible doubt as to the constitutionality of these conditions. Finally, the issues of the compulsory or non-compulsory inclusion of candidates from Hellenism in the state lists and, secondly, the appointment of one or more constituencies from democratic Hellenism are now, with our proposal, a clear and strong constitutional base. However, the discretionary power of the joint legislator as to whether to adopt them is also omitted. It is clear that the New Democracy Government will follow the relevant legislative initiative which will be tabling the decisions of the Intergroup, always awaiting a spirit of consensus and consensus with all parties. This is what we are doing to date in both difficult and easy, and we will continue to do so as a core of our party's power is to stick to national unity and national understanding, away from divisive logic. Ladies and gentlemen, despite the compromises needed to establish a common cross-party approach, today's debate shows that a great step forward has already been taken and is a bet on all of us in the Greek Parliament, the relevant legislative initiative of the Government to vote unanimously, so that we do not permanently send the image of a toxic confrontation that distorts citizens from participation in politics. With regard to the other proposals for revision and in relation to Rule 54(1) on the proportional electoral system, we must state briefly the following: As has been detailed in the debate in the committee responsible, our prolongation disagrees with the constitutionalisation of the compulsory application of a proportional electoral system in the parliamentary elections. In our view, there should not be this excessive limitation of the common legislator on the choice of a electoral system. In addition, this prevalence of the rhetoric of the Anti-Population Officer - this matter would lead to unregulatedness - cannot be left to a party that receives more than 40% of the voters to govern itself and to need permanent minions, by providing consideration and subjecting to extortion. We have proved as an exception that when national circumstances called it into the crisis, we have not hesitated to cooperate with forces of traditional opponents, such as PASOK and DIMAR, in order to get the country out of the war, in response to our historical responsibility. But at no time today can we consent to the constitutionalisation of chaos. [[At this point, the fourth Vice-President of Parliament, Mr DIMITRIOS VITSAS, takes over the Presidential Chair.]] Let us now refer to the SYRIZA's proposed revision of paragraph 5 in Article 56 on the blockades of election. A seemingly attractive proposal, which introduces a temporary blockage of election for the next parliamentary elections immediately to be held by Members. However, this is true for three consecutive parliamentary periods and, indeed, for at least one thirty-two months. Ladies and gentlemen, it is obviously not for us to re-election the Member for the development of arrogance and nepotism. We know that. It has been seen on several occasions in young, first elected Members. But, in essence, if we look at the last parliamentary periods, none other than the last 2015-2019, is more than thirty-two months. So who, in action, is involved in this proposal? I think of anyone. [[At this point, the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] I shall conclude, Mr President. This is therefore a provision without a practical or legal or political objection. It's a substance-free firework. However, this cannot be a documented review proposal, given the strictness of the Constitution and therefore cannot be accepted. Ladies and gentlemen, I believe that our consensual proposal has clearly emerged in the matter of Greeks voting outside the State and the absence of the need to revise Articles 54 and 56, on the basis of the other proposals made by the Civilian Opposition Officer. Today, I believe that we will do the widest possible consensus on the first national matter. I would like to stress that this is to the credit of the House. We must all preserve the role of the Parliament, the institutions and above all of the Constitution itself, demonstrating to the Greek people and especially to our fellow citizens who live and work abroad, constitutional responsibility. Today they have their eyes on us and they expect us to give them the opportunity, after more than forty-five years, to exercise this top democratic right and finally vote in the next elections. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u284	Thank you very much, Mr President. I do not think there is a clearer and more honest explanation to both the Greek people and the other political forces. I now come to the essence of a number of issues that are going to be decided on today. I'm starting with Article 62. Historically, the Member is protected against any interventions by the executive against him for reasons of feasibility. For this reason, the MP's institutional shield appears for the first time in the French Constitution of 1791, in Bonn's fundamental law in Article 46 and in the Constitution of the United States of America in Article 1(6). That is in response to some colleagues who said that they are not protected anywhere. The first thing the most mature democracies do is that. Almost all the Greek Constitutions - to see it a little historical - provided relevant provisions. From Article 56 of 1844 or Article 63 of 1864. Functionally, Article 62 protects the Member from posterior political persecution of third parties who could intentionally expose him, particularly in order to affect his independence and his freedom of action as a parliament, to serve either their own interests or their interrelated interests. That is the concept of Article 62. We protect the Member in order to protect him from the pressures and compelling of the interlocutory or other opponents who might want to manipulate him. Politics now, Article 62 aims historically to act as an institutional guarantee that reflects the supremacy of legislative power, which is the one directly democratically legitimised by the people. However, this does not, of course, mean that the Member is excluded from the general rule of equality before the law. In the new environment that is created internationally as the democratic institutions grow and grow, the reason for the absolute protection of parliament is missing. This is also reflected in the relevant judgments of the European Court of Human Rights, which, recently, decided to lift parliamentary immunity on matters which were not relevant to the performance of the duties of the Member. We have three decisions: Tsalkitzis vs Greece 2006, Synglidis vs Greece 2010 and Kontova versus Italy 2003. The New Democracy therefore considers that the Members of Parliament's institutional role is adequately safeguarded in the modern environment, when parliamentary immunity concerns not all crimes, but exclusively offences committed in the performance of the duties of the Member. Of course, this assessment is carried out by the House itself, which has the experience, the knowledge to judge unreasonably whether or not it is being concealed or not, behind the persecution of a political person. This protection, in so far as it remains, responds to the populism of various extreme groups - we have experienced it in the past period - which do not often hesitate to excite Parliament, until the people themselves enter it themselves, threaten many times elected representatives of the people by slandering them and reducing their image before the world, so that they can stagger the people's confidence in the political world in a populist way and thereby push the voters to the extremes, namely the active citizens. The citizens themselves, however, must have the main reason, until the last minute and decide when and whether or not they should send a politician to his home. Our review proposal seeks to find a golden and appealing intersection between the impunity of some of the jurors of parliament on the one hand and the misdemeanor of parliamentary and democracy on the other hand, through an equitable punishment. I'm going to the next article now. Article 86 testifies to the credibility of our political system even more than Article 62, because it concerns not only a legislator, but an executive officer or an executive officer, because that is what the Minister does. It must therefore seek to strike a balance between, on the one hand, absolute and unacceptable impunity of executive policies - which undermines citizens' confidence in leadership and the morality of leadership and puts them away from the commons, offends the quality of our democracy, strengthens populism, pushs people towards the extremists - and, on the other, unjustly distorting and absolute punishment that can at times satisfy the entangled popular feeling, but takes away serious people from politics. It discourages managers from making decisions and solving problems, feeling unprotected the next day. It creates an incredible inertia in executive power that gradually leads to the destruction of the country. It identifies politics not with decisions that solve problems, but with a epidermal and ruthless communication. The dynamics of non-judgmental inertia due to fear of punishment rapidly prevents the entire hierarchy of the administration and of course, as assessed by economists, the cost of non-judgmental decisions in Greece is three times the cost of bad decisions. In all aspects of public life, speed, quality, rewarding for society, the state and the nation cease to be an assessment criterion. All this is replaced by a seasonal and prefabricated image. Anyone who decides and takes results usually pays expensive and personally the price. On the contrary, anyone who cares and does not sign, but knows to communicate around TV windows and replaces politics with public relations, gains highest positions of popularity in the consciousness of the people. How expensive does our country still have to pay for the economy, administration, foreign policy and defence, its policy of responsibilities, the responsibilities of politicians or the blind punishment of populists, or the impunity of irresponsible and immoral rulers? In Greece, unfortunately, the criminal responsibility of the ministers and the introduction of proprietary crimes has a deep root. It starts from the first constitutions of 1922, the law of Epidaurus, Troizina, the Constitutions of 1844, 1864, 1927. The use or often misuse of the corresponding provisions has even led to the sprinkling of personalities or tragic ethnic divisions, whether the charges were justified or - as they have been ex post proved - unfounded. I would remind you of a few representative cases about the history which I have presented in more detail in committee. The Minister of Finance of the Government of Koletis was appointed in ‘23, Metaxas, Petrobe, Mavromichalis in ‘24, the Minister of Finance of the Government of Kolettis in ‘24, the Prime Minister Alexandros Koumoundouros, Charilaos Trikoupis, the ministers of the Theotokis, Dragoumis, Tsamados in 1892. The majority of liberals of 1927 referred to the governments of “15-17, the Kaloudes, Spyridon Lamprou and Dimitrio Gounari. In 1933 the reference made by the President of the Republic of Alexandros Zaimis and Eleftherios Venizelos was made. After World War II, Minister Hatzipanos was convicted. In 1965 the referral was made to Constantine Karamanlis and to the Ministers of Protopapadakis, Bournia, Helmi, Marti, Papalegura, as well as to Minister Averov and his State Secretarys. Andreas Papandreou was referred to in 1989 with ministers Koutsogiorgas, Tsovola and Pechos. Constantine Mitsotakis was referred to in 1994, and the phenomenon reached us until today. This historic route is perhaps teaching for both the present and the future, because it shows us not only how useful it is for the Minister to be treated as before the law with ordinary citizens, but also how necessary it is for him to be shielded in the performance of his duties so that he does not work in the hands of his political opponents or other powerful populists or even - even - even outside-institutionally intertwined interests, Article 86(3), leading to fainting of the face, inactivating the political and ultimately, at national, social and political divisions. I believe that the New Democracy's proposal to remove from Article 86(3) the expropriatory deadline for the offences of the ministers, referring to the law, equates the treatment of the ministers with the ordinary citizens before the law, without abandoning the immunisation of the responsible and moral policies that are willing to make decisions on the good of our country. On the other hand, I consider the interpretative statement proposed by SYRIZA to be excessive and dangerous, calling for retroactive force in the search for the criminal liability of a minister. First of all, it runs counter to the fundamental principle of the law ‘nullum cremen, nulla poena sine lege’, i.e. no crime and no penalty shall be imposed if there is no law directly enshrined in the Constitution itself, in Article 7(1). Retrospective prosecution of Ministers for alleged criminal offences in the performance of their duties, committed when this provision did not apply, can legally expose the country, because it runs counter to basic human rights and freedoms, as well as to the current case-law of the Court of Human Rights. We will achieve this if we listen to its needs, the needs of the people who are thirsty for progress and improvement of their lives after ten years of hard struggle with major economic problems that have hit the entire Greek society, but also the perceptions and attitudes that have created this very great crisis. We will achieve this if we all leave yesterday's mindsets on the sidelines and embrace all the Greeks, wherever they are, as long as they too want to offer for our country. As much as in the economy, in technology, in international institutions, globalisation is taking place - and indeed at a rapid pace - so much so, ladies and gentlemen, I think the need for an effective national state, a state that respects and represents the patriotism of citizens, as well as the instability of freedom, security and social cohesion, again emerges. That is why I believe that the road to tomorrow is not just that of economic development. Greece must become a state and this presupposes patriotism, the unity of the Greeks and the consent of the political forces to issues which form the core of a rule of law in the service of citizens. This is nowadays of great value. Contrasts and confrontations between world powers, regional conflicts and interests lead to large migratory flows. In such situations national security is a condition for social cohesion, and we must all respect it in practice. Our country, therefore, needs, in addition to all others, the enlisting of the forces of global Hellenism. Greece must keep in its arms all Greeks, wherever they are: First of all all all the new children studying abroad or leaving in the years of the crisis to gain opportunities for life and success or simply to have a normal life that Greece could not guarantee. But also our older compatriots who left decades ago, but continue to care about Greece, love it, hurt it, support it by acts and watch us very carefully about what we say and what we do. But we too, the political forces of the country, must show that we want the support of this other Greece that lives outside our borders, and one way of with particular semantic and practical value is to make it easier for our fellow citizens to exercise their right to vote, those who are, of course, registered on the electoral lists. The Constitution expressly recognises the right of non-national Greeks to participate in the electoral process and refers to a law which will facilitate them to vote from their home place. This law - as you know - requires a majority of two hundred MPs in the House. It is not a restriction created by the present majority, neither by the current Prime Minister nor by the current Minister for the Interior, since they did not even share the time when this article of the Constitution was drafted in the political scene of the country. In all of us, however, the Members of this Parliament are responsible for creating the shocks that are needed to permanently and celebratingly the majority of the two hundred seats, so that the law can pass through the House to facilitate the Greeks abroad to vote from their homes. The New Democracy's position on this matter was and remains clear. We should make it easier for all Greeks from abroad who are already on the electoral lists or who have the right to register and should vote through the letter of vote. This position, however, on the basis of the existing parliamentary relationships, would not bring together the number of two hundred votes required by the Constitution. The mandate I received from the Prime Minister at the first moment and which I served with enthusiasm was to create the widest possible shocks to enable as many Greeks abroad to vote from their homes. We have had long and painful discussions with all the parties in Parliament and on 22 October we have basically reached a compromise treaty which will facilitate - I believe - several thousand Greeks voting in the next elections in the countries inhabited. Unfortunately, the restrictions imposed by parties do not allow all those on the electoral lists to make use of the beneficial provision. It is clear that there will be our compatriots who will not have this facility. Evidently those who cannot make use of the law, which I feel sure will be voted on by the necessary majority, will be able to exercise their electoral right, as has been foreseen so far, by coming to Greece and voting in the already registered constituencies. It's not what we wanted. This compromise, however, allows us to take a major first step. Ladies and gentlemen, it does not matter who made more compromises. It is important who has proved to be the political will to do so. If today's Civic Opposition wanted to have done so, it was able to take such an initiative in practice during the previous parliamentary term. However, it did not do so, and this absence of actions is extremely eloquent and understandable to Greeks in and out of Greece. During the debate that led to the agreement I referred to on 22 October, some political forces raised issues of unconstitutionality with regard to the restrictions that are being put forward, such as the voters who will be facilitated and who will vote from their homes to have proven real ties with the country. It was therefore necessary, in Article 54, which is revised, to shield the provisions to be included in the law, whose draft was drawn up on the basis of the agreement we have said. Today, a few hours ago, the Ministry of Interior published the final text, following the public consultation that has taken place, with all the improvements and clarifications, which I believe make it easier for everyone to pass this bill on to the full. Based on the proposal for revision, a prerequisite for the exercise of the electoral right of non-national Greeks is the absence time from the country or presence in Greece for a certain period of time in the past. In particular, in the last thirty-five years, the presence of at least two years in the country, regardless of when each Greek citizen has left our country and, at the same time, in the last one or two years, must have made a tax declaration by using points E1, E2, E3 and E9 of the tax declaration. All of this is included in very detailed terms in the regulations which, as I said, have come to the public since the consultation that took place over the last few days. I repeat, therefore, that the specific addition, which Mr Stylianides spoke about earlier, is made in Article 54 of the Constitution to shield the restrictions put forward by certain parties, as far as the facilities are concerned for the non-national Greeks are concerned. Many of these are potential and not mandatory - and quite rightly so - because it is a shame that the mistake made in the previous Review has been made, to commit irrevocably new parliamentary majorities to what they will do in the future. The lessons and sufferings of the past I think we should take them for the future. Ladies and gentlemen, I would like you to vote in favour of this addition to Rule 54. We must listen to society and compose opinions. We must create shocks. Clearly, consensus is in no way an end in politics. In fact, several times, the course in areas and reforms prove to be collisional. The parties have an ideological and political identity. Ideological separatist lines, although they have been reduced, are clearly existential. But when political decisions concern basic democratic rights of citizens of a national nature, consensus is a self-evident duty and shows the extent of the responsibility of each of us to the citizens who have trusted us not only in their vote, but in their progress and their families, in the quality of their lives. Then, the greatest ideological opponents must find agreements and shocks for the good of the Greek people. In conclusion, I would just like to add that in the year 2020 we are shortly called upon to do what it did a hundred and fifty eight years ago, in 1862, Zaimis as Minister of the Interior, when by law he allowed Greeks abroad to vote. We are therefore called upon to do what the twenty-six of the eight countries of the European Union are doing, but also what our predecessors did a century and a half ago, believing that this is how we take an important first step towards the justice of our compatriots who want to participate in the country's commons, but not all have the opportunity to do so by travelling to our home country during the elections. For all these reasons, I address a call to all the political forces of the place to vote unanimously to complete Article 54, as we all have to vote next week on the law to facilitate the vote of Greeks abroad. It will be a strong message of responsibility for all political parties. It will be a strong message of fundamental credibility of the political system, a strong message that the country wants to keep strong ties with them, especially now, in the great effort to regenerate it. And the most direct political bond in a democracy is the vote of every citizen, which we will do, by changing and amending Article 54 of the Constitution and by voting unanimously - I hope - the law on the vote of the Greeks abroad next week. Thank you very much.

The principle of equality of citizens is therefore violated. Article 54 is very characteristic of the way our Constitution is structured. It defines the way and not the content. It stipulates, for example, that the electoral system, the number of regions will be defined by law, that the number of MPs per electoral district will be determined by a presidential decree. This means, of course, that by definition, it creates a system which is volatile. This means that, despite any security differences, it is absolutely possible - and we have been living this very long in recent years - to change the electoral system very regularly. In fact, this is what it says in its name, to further explain the rules in force in Article 86. What does Article 86 provide for? That it is only ministerial offences in the narrow sense of the term and not those committed simply in the exercise of ministerial duties. By adding this interpretative statement, it is our intention to clarify that the latter are persecuted under common legislation, that is, in the same way, as for all citizens, because we believe there is no reason to protect the ministers from the Constitution for an illegal act that cannot be linked to their duties. The government of SYRIZA considered that there should be a narrow, strict, inappropriate interpretation of what constitutes a ministerial offence and what a crime against the occasion of the exercise of ministerial duties and the latter being criminally tall for the Minister as it is for each of us. Our aim is primarily to avoid scandals and gradually restore citizens' confidence in our political system. We do not introduce a new rule of law. Which means that the few things that will be launched will not last, while this attempt involves the great risk of this change happening, violently, when no one expects it. Of course, this is extremely worrying for everyone who loves their homeland and does not want to be led to new adventures, and they do not want to be forced to leave it either, or their children, even if they have the opportunity, precisely because they love it. Now known as law, the introduction of the Constitution by the citizens, the only real change that, if any, would lead Greece to the right path and out of the crisis, could have the following stages: Firstly, public consultation on the Constitution begins by tabling the views of all the citizens of interest, as is the case with bills. Secondly, the preparation of public consultation by a group of specialised legal experts in order to group the proposals and put questions to a referendum. Thirdly, a referendum is held to decide on the change in the constitution and its main axes. Fourthly, the election of a constituent assembly to prepare the draft of the new constitution according to the axes of the referendum. Fifth, final referendum on ratification of the draft constitution. In this regard, perhaps some claim that the process is utopian, simply because they do not know that this is exactly what happened in Iceland, where its citizens after an exemplary consultation voted to introduce the country's new constitution. The fact that he protected them from the 2008 crisis is enough to convince us of his correctness. In addition, many other constitutions, such as Switzerland, France and Spain, require that the people vote directly in a referendum. It is therefore a disgrace for the mother of the Republic, for Greece, to be so anachronistic that the citizens are still on the margin of political life as a ravage of politicians. With regard to the proposal on the revision of Articles 62 and 86 of the Constitution, I have to say the following. In view of the morality of politics, Articles 62 and 86 of the Constitution have been chosen for revision. In the context of equal treatment for all, regardless of their status, i.e. if it is a Member, a Minister or anything else, the views that negatively criticise these provisions have been multiplied. They are described as anachronistic, unrestrictedly favorable and unfair because they offend the legal sense of equal treatment for everyone before justice. The parliamentary practice has so far shown that Parliament is hesitant to use these provisions. Our colleague, a special speaker of the Greek Solution, pointed out that between 1975 and 2005 approximately a thousand applications were made for the waiver of immunity from which only five were accepted. It is a figure indicative of the necessity of collecting a revision of the provisions. Unfortunately, it has been subjected to an inappropriate anti-democratic attack on the category of selectivity of the time period, which certainly did not happen since these were just the statistics available to us. It is really regrettable for Parliament and the Republic in our country, but above all for the government of the state of service. In any case, we have asked for new information so that it is more up-to-date. In any event, this leads to the fact that the parliamentary post must not be justified or justified in order for the Member to have immunity for criminal offences. He will not only be prosecuted for a vote which he gave to the House, for his speech within the House and for his opinion in the parliamentary term, since both his speeches and his opinions are not affected by any specific person with defamation. Further and proportionately, Article 86 of the Constitution requires not only revision but also abolition as a whole, so that everyone can be treated in a uniform manner and apply the laws to all. However, in view of the possibility of revising only specific paragraphs of this resolution, as decided by the House, it is preferable not only to abolish the short deadline but also to allow all parliamentary parties to submit a proposal for a preliminary examination against a minister. A smaller majority must therefore be provided for in order to establish a special parliamentary committee to carry out a preliminary examination, such as 1/3 of the total number of Members, and the decision to refer is reasonable to be taken by a majority of Members present who must not be less than 2/3 of the total Members. In order to avoid doubts at an interpretative level, it is also proposed to introduce an interpretative declaration on the possibility of the investigating body to refer the case-file to the House, when the latter, up to the end of the interrogation, considers that there is now sufficient evidence to establish criminal liability of a government person. So what is happening today in the House, that is to say someone in the Committee of Inquiry on NOVARTIS, who had never been examined by a specialist before, would not have been able to examine it by us in the committee without any idea of how the investigation is being conducted, so that this party, which does not have the right people in the committee of inquiry, would be in a very difficult position. It's unfair, and it's gonna have to stop. In continuing, as regards the proposal on the revision of Articles 54 and 56 of the Constitution, I have the following to say. The current constitution allows the joint legislature to define the electoral system for the election of the parliament. Irrespective of the arguments developed by the supporters of the various electoral systems, we again emphasise that the simple analogue is, in our view, the best electoral system. It is also the simplest, clearest and fairest electoral system, since it represents the will and vote of Greek citizens in the most representative way. In this context, the mere analogous application, i.e. the absolute proportion of votes and seats, is seen by us as the best way of showing Members and sharing seats. Furthermore, the simple analogue system is entirely consistent with the 3% entry limit, in order for a party to enter the House. This is how the will of the Greek citizens gains the necessary cohesion and power within Parliament, after being translated into a parliamentary party. Furthermore, we are certainly in favour of the exercise of the electoral right by voters who are outside the Greek territory. The vote of the Greek Democrats concludes the electorate and practically recognises the Greek conscience that Greeks have. The vote of the Greek Parliament supports the genuine representation of the Greeks in the Greek Parliament. Let us not forget that the Greeks of foreigners love Greece much more than all of us, which I have seen many times. However, appropriate conditions should be provided for, but also for security controls to ensure the conditions for the proper exercise of the electoral right of the deportees, depending on the fair representation and electoral power of all the parties. Finally, and with regard to the blockades of Members' election, the parliamentary post must be declassified from any concept of profession and its operational character strengthened. In this context, there should be a definitive block of election as long as a person has been elected three times as a Member. In other words, no one should ever be allowed to be elected for the fourth time. Thank you very much.

One of the main issues raised during the revision process in this direction is the question of parliamentary immunity, Article 62 of the Constitution, as well as the question of 'departmental responsibility', Article 86 of the Constitution. For Rule 62, the parliamentary majority had chosen to maintain the immunity of the Member as an institutional guarantee for the free and uninterrupted exercise of the duties of the Member, but limited solely to his political action as a Member. This addition further strengthens Parliament's obligation - even in accordance with the current constitution - to lift parliamentary immunity in respect of offences not related to parliamentary office. In the same direction as the further modernisation of the institutional framework, on the basis of the limitation of Members' privileges, the proposal of the parliamentary majority for Article 86 of the Constitution, with the complete abolition of the deadline for the Second Session, is initiated. In fact, this means that the offences of the Ministers fall within the normal limitation for all Greek citizens.
