ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u403	Ladies and gentlemen, my thoughts regarding both the limitations of the parliamentary immunity of Article 62 and the ministerial responsibility of Article 86, which I shall develop, are based on two basic pillars for our democracy: on the one hand, the non-extortive criminalisation of political life by extra-institutional circles, on the other, on the defence of the right to judicial protection and fair trial of third parties affected by acts and omissions of the respective political people, MPs and ministers. In particular, with regard to Rule 62, the immunity of Members in our time does, at first glance, give rise to suspicion and reservations about citizens. It has been established as a privilege and disproportionate exception from the principles of the rule of law and equality before the law. With today's Review, it is necessary to make it clear that immunity does not mean that the Member is unavoidable and unreserved for a generalised set of illegal criminal conduct, which is why immunity must go through in a closer causal link with the parliamentary status. In our country, the substantial distortion of fellow parliamentary solidarity has resulted in, as has shown, a set of scientific studies, being granted with dire permissions from the House that allow criminal prosecution. This, of course, results in Greece being subject to serious convictions from the European Court of Human Rights. Therefore, it is not a question of abolishing the immunity of Members, but of rationality and efficiency of our legal and safeguarding our democracy. In this light it should be demonstrated by all of us in this Chamber that the irresponsibility of the Member for his opinion or his vote is valid, unnecessarily and necessary. Solution, in our modern democratic state organization, can be the limitation of immunity purely to matters which are raised and dealt with by the House of the real and legal circumstances of each prosecution. It is also worth keeping in this direction the functionality of the importance of existing forecasts that do not remove any criminality, as mistakenly there may be the distorting impression of the common sense. On the contrary, they inhibit it as long as the person's parliamentary status is in force, even if the offences were played earlier or during that status. It is also worth making it clear to the citizens that any civil responsibilities still do not enjoy the slightest, even inhibiting, tolerance today. With regard to the judgments of the European Court of Human Rights, guidelines for our common European constitution are the judgments: 'Cordova v Italy', concerning the former President of the Italian Republic, Francesko Kosiga, as then a Member under the immunity of the respective article 68 of the Italian Constitution, the case of 'Tsalkidiki v Greece' in respect of acts of the former mayor of the Member of the European Parliament and of the Council against Greece', which, in essence, concerned with the criminality of a family diligence and child communication dispute. These cases at the European Court of Justice have come to the fundamental conclusion that Article 62 of the Greek Constitution, interpreted in the light of paragraph 1 of Article 6 of the European Convention on the Rights of Human Rights for a fair trial, allows the Greek Parliament to refuse the waiver of immunity of a Member only if the acts attributed to him are undoubtedly linked under the established conditions of the principle of proportionality and with a strong justification purely for his parliamentary activity. In other words, Article 6 of the Convention requires the House, in any particular request to waive the immunity of one of its Members, to examine the existence of a link between the acts and the parliamentary duties complained of and to give reasons for not revoking the immunity of the Member only if such a manifest and proportionate relevance is established. Thus, the fundamental right of citizens to legal protection is not being violated by the courts, but also by Members of Parliament, who are not using the parliamentarians' institution abusively, so that they remain unpunished for criminal offences, unjustified by their duties, in contrast to the opposing intimidation of the MP and his abusive persecutions. That is what we want to guarantee, ladies and gentlemen, today with our choice in this particular article. With regard to Article 86, I would like to say that the present constitutional review of the responsibility of Ministers is, for the New Democracy, the mark put forward, I would say, of the public interest, of the political ethos and of the new legal transparency that it wants to establish in the resumption of the state reorganisation of our country, especially, after the tenth memorandum years, and even partly of the disguised covert cases of corruption, infringibility and inappropriate customer relations of the executive with various economic extra-institutional interests. Prime Minister Kyriakos Mitsotakis, at an earlier time in this Review, as a Member, had sought in previous parliamentary times the judgment of Article 86 as being reviewed by the signature of fifty Members, collecting the insecurity of his colleagues in times of intense populism, with the impressions of political persecution snatching the real need for fair control and transparency, without, in fact, interference with the final judge of the substance of the cases, who is no other than judicial service. Our proposal is, in my opinion, clear and concrete. For the ministers, the exceptionally short amortization period should be abolished for the exercise of Parliament's powers of referral to common justice, as we are proposing, with the decision to support them positively or negatively from an enlarged judiciary consisting of high-ranking judicial officers. Also, the whole process of drawing up the finding and its adoption by Parliament should be completed as soon as possible, with the mention of a specific reasonable time, so that no case has been drawn up for a long time. The special responsibility of the Ministers is a constitutional provision which is always in the Greek Constitutions from 1844 to our days. With the 2001 Review, the relevant regulation of the Constitution of 1975 became indeed more stringent in terms of its procedure and substance, so it was voted in favour of two hundred and sixty-eight Members, followed by any defects made by Law 3126/2003 on the responsibility of the Ministers. However, the procedural formulation of the conditions for the limitation of offences, with the early dissolution of the House in the past, was, in fact, interpreted as an institutional overlap. This therefore makes it necessary to introduce additional guarantees. Such arrangements exist in other developed constitutions of Western states, in many of which, as in the United States, parliament is responsible not only for initiating the relevant procedure but also for judging the case as a court. Without such provisions, it cannot be ruled out in a country and the necessary political decisions that would otherwise be converted into criminal proceedings, at least for the multi-invoked breach of duty for each public authority. Ladies and gentlemen, the rational reform of Article 86 of the Constitution on the criminal liability of politicians, by removing the privilegedly unacceptable short-term and procedural limitations and by introducing a ten-year deadline so that governmental persons are not bound by political persecution, seems - and I hope - to ensure that the majority of the parties are accepted as an additional restoration movement of state institutions towards our society, indicating the institutional and operational substance of the necessary provisions. With these thoughts, thank you for having been patient to listen to me.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u407	The revision of the provisions of the modules we are debating today, ladies and gentlemen, is necessary for the institutional reconstruction of the country towards the regeneration of the political system and the deepening of the democratic functioning of all functions, with a view to strengthening popular sovereignty. The first change, therefore, is the reduction of the Member's impunity, whose application has led to criminal immunity. The parliamentary immunity referred to in Article 62 of the Constitution is dictated by the real need for the Member of Parliament to have freedom of opinion, speech and political action, in order for a post and without fear of persecution to respond to his public role. On the other hand, the Member should not abuse his or her parliamentary position and status in order to commit offences under the ordinary criminal law which have nothing to do with his or her political activity, nor with his public role and public status. That is why it is proposed to revise the provision of Article 62 of the Constitution, which, as it is currently stated, is abusive invoked and implemented. For example, Members who commit offences under the influence of alcohol or illegal physical violence should not be protected and should not be prosecuted for having immunity, thereby giving populists the pretext for confiscation and politics and policies. In what direction should this provision be reviewed? For offences not related to their parliamentary status or to their parliamentary action, to be prosecuted as ordinary citizens, without requiring a parliament's permission to prosecute. They must therefore be accounted for in the criminal courts in accordance with common procedural rules, without requiring the Parliament's decision, taken by an absolute majority of the Members present to prosecute the Member. In the same direction, the revision of Article 86 on the responsibility of Ministers, which gave the sense - not unjustly - of the unjustified privileged criminal treatment of the ministers in relation to ordinary citizens and, at the same time, gave the impression that a protected chamber of corruption has been created. The provision of Article 86, as currently in force, sets out a short amortisation deadline which, in conjunction with the application of enhanced proportional systems, gave Parliament a genuinely absolute majority and provided that the reference of the member of the government was necessary by an absolute majority of the total number of Members, was essentially to the impunity of the members of the government. The SYRIZA proposal therefore abolishes the short deadline, which applies as a time-limit for limitation of the offence and proposes that the limitation period for all citizens should apply. It also introduces an interpretative statement stating that the special criminal procedure under Article 86 applies only to offences committed by the Ministers in the performance of their duties and not to those committed on the occasion which must be prosecuted under the common legislation. Of course, we must point out here that there has only been partial treatment of the consequences of Article 86, with the introduction of the crime of money laundering, as has been established, firstly, in 1993, and as the relevant legislation has subsequently been amended. This is because the serious corruption crimes committed by the ministers are most often subject to the objective nature of the laundering of black money, which is an offence that is always committed on occasion. In many cases - as we know to date - ministers of the past who have been sentenced to prison, criminal prosecutions have been carried out on the basis of the provisions on 'black' money laundering and not on the basis of Article 86 of the Constitution and of the special criminal law which they have, in any event, been adopted by means of a delegated act. I think that both Article 86 and Article 62 are incomprehensible about their revision in this particular direction, which is proposed by the SYRIZA. We should, of course, see this provision here in conjunction with SYRIZA's proposal for a constitutional electoral system, in which the notion of an absolute majority of the total number of Members does not coincide with the votes of the party's parliamentary group which first came to the elections, because a government majority is required to obtain wider inter-party consent. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u436	Thank you, Madam President. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, in June 1975 there were only two consecutive days when they had to determine the modern political history of this place. On 12 June the official request for Greece's full membership of the EEC was lodged, while on a day earlier, on 11 June, the new constitution of the country had entered into force, a constitution which, as usual, was not politically neutral, as it was preparing for the entry of our country into the great European family. The principles of freedom, solidarity, the protection of human dignity, i.e. the values of the Western world and our democratic civilization, prevailed. It was a liberal constitutional text with a progressive sign, open to the challenges of the future. Since then, much water has flowed on the groove of history, yet the 1975 Constitution remains alive and timely. The flexible nature of its constitutional provisions, which allowed its content to be filled, which was always changing to the social and political conditions, contributed to this. In this way, along with the Constitution, a specific political practice was developed, which has sometimes remained faithful to the spirit of the constitutional legislature and has sometimes been subject to political purposes, in violation of the ratio of constitutional arrangements. An example of circumvention, apart from Article 32(4) for the presidential election, is also Article 62 on parliamentary immunity which we are debating today in the context of the fifth thematic section. There has been an abusive application of the parliamentary immunity provision. The report itself did not present any ambiguity or interpretative questions, particularly after the amendment of Rule 83 of the Rules of Procedure. After all, the plenary of the House at the last session dealing with such matters, on 3 October 2019, rightly interpreted and applied the existing provision of Rule 62, because that provision that protects civil life from criminalisation does not cover either the previous indifferent activity of the Member or offences regardless of his parliamentary or political activity. So it was a mistaken parliamentary practice that sometimes underwent a misguided conception of a syndicate that made this provision problematic, and that the country should be condemned by the Court of Human Rights. On the other hand, Article 86(3) on the prosecution proceedings against a Minister was the black point of the Constitution, since the planned amortization deadlines have always produced suspicions of limitation and impunity. At a time when political morals are normalized, this suffocating framework for the birth of the criminal prosecution was most privileged for the political staff of the country. And he gave the occasion to dark and anti-political forces for unfair attacks that poisoned public life, cultivating the humblest popular instincts. Today, with the Constitutional Review, the political system restores its credibility and shields democratic institutions and transparency, because transparency requires unnecessarily respect for the principle of rigour in the interpretation and application of the provisions concerning political persons. I now come to the ninth theme. I had planned to speak in relation to the vote on the motions for resolutions, but questions were raised by both the SYRIZA Parliamentary spokesman and also by the Members we heard, particularly in relation to the simple proportional nature and how it is applied to the results of Spain and Sanchez. In relation to the proposal on 'constitutionalisation' of mere analogy, as it stands in the interpretative statement, we must point out that our country has a long culture and tradition of an enhanced analogue system since the late 1950s. Also, almost all post-transitional governments under normal conditions were founded on a comfortable, compact and disciplined parliamentary majority. This majority ensured stability, cohesion, undisturbed longevity and prosperity. This has helped modernise and strengthen the country, but also deepen and resilience to our democratic institutions. However, there is another dimension of the issue. In other European states that have more proportional systems, the constitutional framework allows for the search for a government for a long period of time or even provides for a minority government. Contrary to Greece, due to the historical load of Julians, the system of exploratory orders is strict and typical. It requires the formation of the government in ten to fifteen days. In this context, the revision of provisions 41 and 37 should therefore also be proposed in order to facilitate the formation of coalition governments by optimising the process and strengthening the powers of the President of the Republic. In fact, simply proportionally with the proposal to elect the President of the Republic from the people would create an explosive mixture of political instability. I will not go any further into the analysis of this subject which was debated yesterday, but because, in fact, there were concerns - they were expressed shortly before - I would like to say that life will show that the practice already being followed in this post of persons with greater authority, often from an opposite political standpoint, will be established as a strong political tradition and a customary element of constitutional reality, which will determine the decisions of political leaders. Let us now see what is happening in European countries with electoral systems closer to the simple analogue, such as Spain that has been mentioned. There in the last year we had two elections and a total of four election procedures in equal years. We had government, uncertainty and political unrest. Even today there is a paralytic deadlock, because the socialists, despite their cooperation with the Podemos, are still a minority, so the great country of the Iberian Peninsula is burning from a political, social and economic crisis. And, as always, political instability favours the anti-political and anti-democratic forces, so that the extreme right in Spain in the last elections exceeds 15%. So the simple analogue that you so fascistically restored to Spain has brought far-right, fascism and racism back. If, therefore, these occur in analogue countries, one can easily consider the catastrophic consequences for Greece. It is what Mr Tsipras said, 'good late than never', but unfortunately we are now at 'never'. There is no "slow" yet, it is not the process. We've been in the conversation for years. In conclusion, I would like to say that at the beginning of 2019 I read a statement of SYRIZA about the constitutional review entitled "because above all it is a question of equality and democracy". For the time being, I thought it was just analogous or a vote of the homosexuals. Reading more carefully, however, I found that the SYRIZA Department of Migration and Refugee Policy called for the revision of Article 102 of the Constitution in order to add the explicit reference to the electoral right of foreign immigrants in local government elections. It makes sense and accepts sensitivity and I imagine that there is sensitivity from all of us to the Greeks of the family, especially to those who include all four hundred and fifty thousand scientists who were forced to leave our country in the years of the crisis. So I think I should be allowed to borrow this comprehensive and appropriate title to describe in three words the justification for the new paragraph of Article 54: everything is a question of equality and democracy. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u460	Thank you, Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen, everyone shapes their public attitudes according to and with the experience it has gained from participation in public life. So having served my city for many years from the mayor's honorary position, I would like to bring you my experience, as well as many other former colleagues from the criminalisation of political life. Each mayor is invited daily to make decisions that affect interests, interests sometimes established and powerful. In the case of a major city, in fact, the object of its decision may be appreciated in some tens of millions of euros. That is why we see the mayor haunting the courts for many years after the end of their term of office. I can assure you that this is the vast majority of cases for mayors who have done their duty honestly and conscientiously, for officials who have not been able to submit to the pressures of interests and have done so, knowing that they were not covered by any immunity, by any privilege. But, ladies and gentlemen, is the term 'private privilege' correct, which has prevailed in the field of parliamentary immunity and the criminal responsibility of the ministers? I believe that it is a term which can lead to misunderstandings because it leaves the impression that it introduces inequality between ordinary citizens on the one hand and Members and Ministers on the other. This is not true, because the sizes we compare are by definition unlike. So the principle of equality is not applied here. And it does not apply, because the so-called privilege is not intended to protect the Member or the Minister as a person, as a private person, but to the function he performs. It is an institutional guarantee, which aims to ensure that the Minister or the Member of Parliament performs his duties solely in the public interest, that is to say, free from the fear that he can once be criminally prosecuted because of political feasibility or personal retaliation for acts that he has committed as a public official, not of course, for offences he has committed as a private person. It is true that public opinion has developed a justified disapproval of this special scheme, but for different reasons. In the case of Members, it was reasonable that parliamentary immunity had been abused, that is, that it had protected Members from the criminal prosecution for acts which have nothing to do with their parliamentary duties. It was so intense that the political class, on the initiative and responsibility of the PASOK, responded by establishing the procedure laid down in Rule 83 of the Rules of Procedure of the House. The distinction between acts which are closely linked to the exercise of the parliamentary duties and those for which immunity is to be granted and those which are not connected on the other hand actually today adopted by the New Democracy and by the SYRIZA. We, as a Change Movement, have tabled a radical proposal which goes even further than the reversal of the procedure, so that the Member of Parliament does not enjoy immunity by definition, so that it may be up to Parliament to lift it. We propose that there should be no immunity first of all, but that the Member himself should ask Parliament to bear the burden of proving that his persecution is politically motivated and, if he is convinced of Parliament, that immunity should be activated then alone. The current system has the defect that no one bears personal responsibility to provoke the waiver of immunity and because responsibility is spread to everyone, collective solidarity ultimately prevails, because no one wants to be the bad guy. In our proposal, we now oblige the Member to take the initiative himself and the responsibility not to hide behind the misguided solidarity of his colleagues. We send out the message that the House is not a closed guild that protects its own members. With regard to the criminal responsibility of the ministers, we must look elsewhere at the main cause of the popular reaction, particularly to fatigue, which has caused public opinion by the constant criminalisation of political life, which, in the end, means that ordinary citizens are completely confused. They hear signs and monsters of all kinds of scandals, but eventually they find that, under all of this, political parties are simply seeking to destroy their political opponents morally and politically. Here, in other words, there is no such issue of favourable treatment for the Minister, because, as has already happened on several occasions, the Minister can also be in a more unfavourable position than the citizen, due to a change in the parliamentary majority. What, above all, has annoyed the Greek people is the manipulation of the award of criminal justice for political purposes. The only issue that raises a suspicion of unequal treatment is the short-lived, but it has been demonstrated by the evolution of political life, the amortization of Article 3. However, this cannot lead us to the complete abolition of the extinction deadline, because this constitutes an unconditional surrender to constitutional populism, because for every offence, which is generally simply to be entered into with the exercise of ministerial duties, that is to say, even on the occasion of criminal offences, the former Minister will live for twenty years with the axe of criminal prosecution hanging above his head. As to the offences in the exercise of duties, the ax will be held by the House. As far as crimes are concerned, we shall have the public prosecutors' authority with a similar degree of discretion. If that's not the criminalization of political life, then what is it? Ladies and gentlemen, do we realize the price of this populism? Should we not learn to ask ourselves which Minister will be found to put his signature on a politically controversial decision? Are we ready to see ministers bring the House's conventions and individual administrative acts in order to put their blame on them? Because that will be the price of constitutional populism. Ladies and gentlemen, as mayor, I have signed countless administrative acts, for which I might be able to find criminally accountable by 2039 - I see that you are, unfortunately, laughing, but that is what it is - to have the fate of the mayors, who have spoken to you at the outset, those who haunt the rooms of the criminal courts. Let us not afford the same fate to the ministers. It is the duty of the joint legislator to intervene, in order to set a deadline for expropriation, to be longer than the one laid down in the Constitution, but clearly, shorter than the twenty-year period for some felony. For the record, however, we must stress something about Article 86 of the Constitution. Unfortunately, there is a broad view that the 2001 Review was a product of co-responsibility between the New Democracy and PASOK in order to cover each other's sins. However, with the 2001 Review, the regulation was made by almost all sides in almost all aspects of the stricter. Andreas Loverdes has mentioned this in detail as the rapporteur for our parliamentary group. Let's go to Article 54. We have, from the outset, supported four basic stables: the right to vote for those on the electoral lists, namely in the polls, the vote being equivalent to that of the other voters, the right to at least vote and to adopt a system of electoral districts abroad or part of the roll-call vote. It is positive, on behalf of the government, to seek consensus. However, this consensus cannot be sought at any price. It must be said here that, with the proposal of the parliamentary majority, we are intervening in the unrevised article 51 through Rule 54 and, indeed, introducing a provision which is too, unduly detailed for a constitutional text. On the contrary, we have proposed a simple wording: 'Law lays down restrictions on the exercise of the electoral right by voters outside the territory, in particular in view of the ties they maintain with the country'. The other articles will be dealt with quite briefly. The simple proportional electoral system, whenever applied in the country, has had disastrous results. Greece does not have the luxury to remain, even for a short time, without central government, because under the government there are no strong administrative structures capable of ensuring the continuity of the administration for those periods during which a government crisis occurs, which often occurs in multi-party governments. This is, of course, largely the case for the permanent introduction of a mere actuarial. After all, our Constitution provides for strict deadlines for forming a government. It does not provide time for substantial consultations, a prerequisite for a substantial agreement on a government programme. According to paragraph 2 of Article 37 of the Constitution, where the process of investigative orders is regulated, within about ten days a government has to be formed. How can you get time to build a government co-operation program? I am firmly opposed, ladies and gentlemen, to the introduction of restrictions on parliamentary terminology. We will not combat either the client system or the family rule. We are not allowed to take the road to those who, on the pretext, consider the electorate capable of electing them over time. On the contrary, we need to open up the game wide, so that we can give others the opportunity to fight, guaranteeing them equal opportunities, if we want to open up the political system to young people, see what needs to be done, for example, with the system of highlighting the candidates of the party or with the cross of preference or even with the change of the electoral system, for example by introducing smaller electoral regions. That's more and more. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u462	Ladies and gentlemen, the special 'departmental responsibility' rule is a constitutional provision, which has been in Greek Constitutions since 1844 and has maintained approximately the same form until 2001. But why do we need a special 'duty of ministers' regulation? Why, then, all modern Western states have similar provisions? Would criminal politicians have the imprisonment or otherwise, would any politician take the slightest decision, fearing that he would be persecuted as a criminal for nothing, even for his most insignificant decision? The special 'departmental responsibility' rule prevents, to some extent, the possible legalisation of political life. The Constitution entrusts the law with "demand of Ministers" details. Today, the relevant law is Law No.3126/2003, which reiterates the distinction between, on the one hand, offences committed in the performance of the duties of the Minister and, on the other hand, within the jurisdiction of the House and the Special Court and, on the other hand, for which we are discussing today in the light of the Constitutional Review and on the other, offences which have not been committed in the performance of the duty of the Minister and which are the responsibility of the common justice, namely criminal courts where each of us can be referred. Joint conviction of SYRIZA Members and Members, as well as of the former Prime Minister, Alexis Tsipras, is that the very term "in the exercise of duties" needs further clarification, which is why we consider it necessary to introduce an interpretative statement. In this respect, ladies and gentlemen, especially of the New Democracy, it appears that the current President of the Court of Auditors, John Sarmas, who in his article on August 5, 2019, presents it in the Minutes - just makes an interpretation of what it means "in the performance of the Minister's duties". [[At this point, the Member, Mrs Elisabeth (Bette) Scoofa, presents for the minutes the above article, which is in the archives of the Secretariat Department of the Department of Stenography and Practice of the Parliament.]] Mr Sarmas was chosen following a unanimous decision of the current Ministerial Council and a recommendation by the current Minister of Justice. The point is, you're not even listening to Mr. Sarma. With the 2001 ‘Consensual Review’, the 1975 Constitution's 'departmental responsibility' regulation was made with considerable stricter points of view, allowing, however, the short limitations and the tooling of the Constitution's provisions, the limitation of cases which shocked public opinion. A characteristic example of the affairs of Vatopedi and structured bonds in the period 2007-2009, where the then parliamentary majority of the New Democracy did not, of course, want to prosecute themselves and evaluate criminal evidence, which justice was referred before the House and involved officials of the then government. The parliament closed early in the middle of general popular outcry. However, the conditions for the limitation of offences, which justice had identified, were artificially closed down and developed. So what does SYRIZA suggest? First of all, it needs to be clarified that any offences committed by the members of the government, which have been committed in an obvious expansion, away from the exercise of their duties, are tried by the criminal courts in our country and are not treated in a particular way, for example, if a minister is struggling with restaurant bushes and cross-crops. The current paragraph 1 of Article 86 of our Constitution reads as follows: 'Only Parliament has the power to prosecute those who have been or have been members of the government or of the State Secretary for Criminal offences which have been committed in the performance of their duties'. This expression "in the exercise of their duties" can be interpreted in two ways: It can be interpreted very closely, that is, by the law of "responsibility of Ministers" and not by the Criminal Code, the offences of the members of the Government and of the State Secretarys, which have been committed within the framework of their duties and are relevant to their duties, for example, a public project decision, which clearly favours a particular construction company. Also, this term can be interpreted very widely, namely that the offences, which have simply coincided with the period of duty of the ministers and have some causal link with the exercise of these duties, such as, for example, when a minister leads too quickly to the Maximos Mansion to meet the Prime Minister, but violates all the bright signals in his course. Does that remind you of something? Even more serious, when the Minister intervenes, without any personal benefit, in favour of his friend a big businessman to a Minister of another portfolio, indicating and achieving the adoption of legislation which is favourable to his friend, concealing the evidence which sets out the specific treatment of the operator and in particular by means of legislation. Things are getting worse if, in the previous example, the first Minister receives financial support, i.e. bribes, as we appear to have in the case of 'NOVARTIS'. If the second very wide interpretation, without the introduction of the interpretative statement into our Constitution, is chosen, all of the offences that have, at least, a seeming link to the duties of the perpetrator of the Minister or State Secretary, will benefit from the special treatment under the law'since there is no responsibility'. On the contrary, with the interpretative provision proposed by us SYRIZA's MPs and MEPs, we are dissociating the offences which have an externally apparent relevance to the exercise of these duties. SYRIZA therefore proposes to vote for an interpretative statement, which reads as follows: In the true sense of this article, the offences referred to in paragraph 1, which are dealt with and judged under the special law'substantially responsible', do not include those committed simply in the exercise of ministerial duties. With the help of this interpretative tool, it is limited the scope to work out the provisions of our Constitution and defines a specific way of approximateing the rules on the responsibility of the rulers, excluding interpretative structures forever and closing loopholes which some in the future may think of opening up. Ladies and gentlemen, because of the prevalence of your party, the New Democracy, most of the proposals of the previous parliament, were not received, perhaps because of some sense of comfort and - why not? - arrogance enjoyed by your parliamentary group, which has the ability to vote alone on the provisions it wishes. Do not, however, get carried away from this unfounded convenience, because SYRIZA's proposal is wise and measured, as it will work beneficially both in distinguishing what assumptions can be perceived as offences of common criminal law and what must be judged by the provisions 'nominal responsibility'.

In organising a democratic state, the notion of the electoral system is the main way of organising and exercising political power. Since people's will and sovereignty are the basis of the constitution, the electoral system must act on the basis of them. There are three categories of electoral systems: the majority, proportional and mixed. Fourteen different variants of the above systems have been applied in Greece. In the forty-five years of post-population seven different electoral systems were implemented, and the first six of them were based on the structure of the enhanced analogy of 1958. Of course, all these laws did not provide for the strengthening of proportionality, but for strengthening the first party, usually putting an obstacle to the rest. In our country, therefore, the electoral system was unfair and inequality was debatable for smaller parties. Since the Migration to date, with six different systems, the elections have been held. Although post-population was the long period of democratic political stability in the country, which is certainly linked to current electoral laws, we have not been spared in bankruptcy as well. However, the period of crisis and bankruptcy have brought everything down. They brought to the fore the need for cooperation, creating a new culture. Our political system in conditions of simple analogy requires a surplus of political and cultural maturity for substantial shocks, for partnerships, limiting the demagogic rhetoric of smaller and larger parties. From an institutional point of view, the most anticipated protagonist of the results of the recent elections, as well as the previous and previous elections, was undoubtedly the electoral system. In this respect, this is not a mere influence of electoral will, as is the case with other electoral systems. Such an electoral system leads to a multiple distortion of the election result, which is characterised, in fact, by institutional absurdity, at the same time is deeply undemocratic and manifestly unconstitutional. Anti-democratic in individual parties, due to current political data. I am referring to the election premium of the first party, which in the current version is fifty seats and unfavourable treatment of coalitions and is much more problematic than the original version, forty seats with the previous Scandalides law, which could also be taken by parties coalitions. This counter-democracy lies in the violation of the principle of equality of vote on the first party, as the election gift of the fifty seats is awarded without any criterion, both in terms of the percentage and in terms of the difference of the first and second parties. Anti-constitutional, as judged by a detailed and substantiated opinion by Aristo-Venesiou as President of the Scientific Council of the Parliament, similar to the position of Nikos Alvizatou, invoking the relevant case-law of the court, according to which it is not possible in a democratic regime for the judiciary to have the power to control on which terms and conditions may cooperate parties if they have joint programmes and if their cooperation is reasonable or appropriate. These issues are political and together with others they are taken into account by the people, the only person responsible under the first Article of the Constitution. However, at this point, the system of control of constitutionality in our country, as we know, suffers, because we do not have preventive control by a constitutional court, but only after elections by the court. On the contrary, with the permanent proportional electoral system, the simple proportional system, there would be no possibility of overturning the principle of parity. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u490	Thank you, Madam President. Mr Ministers, ladies and gentlemen, the process of Revision of the Constitution is a top democratic process and not an exercise of party or micro-political purposes. Unfortunately, the majority of the previous House showed a short-sighted logic and did not see the process of Constitutional Review as an opportunity to open a window in the future. Through the deepening of the democratic institutions, the strengthening of the credibility of the political system, the exemption from harassment and burdens which prevent the country from moving forward, idealism has prevailed, and Article 16 has not been revised, so that our country is one of the few countries which forbids the operation of non-state universities. Some do not see tomorrow, but they do not see what is happening today either. Because if they were to see, they would realize what is happening in Cyprus, which has become an international centre of university education in the wider Mediterranean region, with multiple benefits for its economy. The establishment of non-state universities would therefore benefit the Greek economy, as well as students, who would not leave abroad. Also, Greece would become a pole of attraction for students from all over the world. The Constitutional Review, therefore, is of an inappropriate and limited nature. The review process, ladies and gentlemen, is subject to rules. The top of these rules is the creation of a climate of consensus, and the Government of New Democracy gave substantial examples of writing in this direction. The intention to create a climate of consensus was clearly reflected in the Prime Minister's initiative to give the migrant Greeks the right to vote, which is linked to the revision of Article 54. Through a synthetic process and mutual retreats, the whole bill was formed, with the aim of gaining broad support and consent from all the political forces in Parliament. With the right to vote and to the Greeks abroad, Greece grows, enters a new era. The Parliament will now express the nation as a whole in the most substantial way. There was a cross-party agreement, which provides that Greeks will have the right to vote less than thirty-five years from home, provided that two years have elapsed in Greece during this period. The main thing is that the principle of the equal vote of Greeks abroad, which will be measured in the final result, and also the right to exercise the electoral right in the local embassies and consulates of Greece, is laid down. It is particularly important that this provision now falls within the constitutional charter. It is particularly important, too, that the consensus be reached on the disconnection of the election of the President of the Republic since elections is held. The constitutionalisation of minimum guaranteed income, which incorporates the principle of a social market economy in the Constitution, which has, as a condition in recent years and in the fundamental European Treaties, been taken into account in the Constitution of the Hellenic Republic. I would like to refer to SYRIZA's proposal that simple analogue, which is not accepted by a large majority of citizens, should be introduced in constitutional terms and conflict with reality itself. Suffice to see the problems facing the municipalities and regions, where the composition of the councils and municipal authorities has emerged through the system of mere proportionality, problems which exist, despite the institutional intervention of the Government of New Democracy, which largely facilitates the concept of governance and the functioning of the municipalities. I spoke before about the need to strengthen the credibility of the political world and the relationship of trust with the citizens, which is essential for the functioning of our institutions and democratic culture. The two revised articles, Article 62 and Article 86, move in this direction. Rule 62 rationalised the framework for the immunity of Members. The immunity of the Member is an institutional guarantee for the performance of his duties only in terms of political activity. This is now clearly defined and without broad interpretations in Article 62. In no case can parliamentary immunity extend to fields which have nothing to do with the parliamentary activity of the Member. From now on, the House, if there is a request from a public prosecutor for a Member's offence, which is not linked to the performance of its duties or to his political activity, is obliged to waive immunity. Since we ourselves must give practical examples of writing, I would remind you that in the previous parliamentary term I had asked for my immunity to be waived and normally to be tried, following a complaint lodged against me and against other members of the Lefkada Municipal Council on the granting of a canteen licence, on the island's beach. The element of strengthening the credibility of the political world, but also of satisfying the citizens' legitimate feelings, is the proposal to revise Article 86. With the proposal of the New Democracy, which I believe gathers the consent of all the political forces, the deadline for the Second Session is to be abolished, on the basis of which a limitation is made. From now on, the offences of the Ministers come under the rules of limitation applicable to ordinary citizens. The politician cannot have distinct or special treatment, and this must be a message to society. Ladies and gentlemen, the review process may not have the scope and potential that Greek society would like. It, however, recommends a step forward and improvement of the constitutional framework and the functioning of the constitution, a step forward which, even in this limited context, came from dialogue, composition and rationalisation. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-21-S1-commons.u492	Thank you very much, Madam President. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, Mr President of the Commission, approaching the end of the top review process of the Constitution today, very important articles, such as the Member's impunity in Article 62, which protects the Member from acts of desirability against him, namely by persecution of third parties, in order to harm him politically and other interests and prevent him from carrying out his duties. Of course the Member is not excluded from the equality rule before the law. Today, our proposal concerns the restriction of parliamentary immunity, with regard to offences outside parliamentary duties and is maintained for those committed in their exercise. The House undertakes to judge realistic and unequivocally the appropriate persecution of the Member. Our proposal does not mean that it does not devaluate parliamentaryism. Instead, it strengthens the credibility of political staff and strengthens the quality of our democratic culture. Also important is Article 86, which deals with the law on the liability of Ministers and which puts an end to a debate many years ago on the different treatment of political persons with regard to the limitation of offences. The reproach that the House is legislating in favour of the Ministers and the New Democracy proposes the abolition of the amortization deadline and, by extension, the forward-looking approach. It is my view that the need to revise this article is necessary both for SYRIZA's proposal and for Mr Tsipras' recent statements, which is why I am not going to stand there, but I am moving forward with my view that the need to revise this article is necessary, because in addition to the rest, it will strengthen public order, the cleansing of public life, transparency and the re-establishment of the confidence of the political system and the politicians. I cannot avoid the temptation to recall that the Prime Minister, Mr Mitsotakis, has taken a clear position and initiatives in favour of abolishing this article since 2008. Let me also remind you that at the time the left-wing parties, namely the coalition and the JRC, refused to support its proposal. But the time came and it turned out that Mr Mitsotakis was right, and now I believe that everyone will give an account of the change that is being imposed. Ladies and gentlemen, today's section includes the most important article 54, which deals with the question of the vote of Greeks living abroad. Of course, the democratic right to vote as such is self-evident, since we all know that it is expressly stated in the Constitution. What it is doing is making it easier for them to exercise this right from their homes and to measure it equally, like all Greeks. Tons of ink have been spilled and endless hours have been spent in the public debate, so that there can be a conclusion and a decision on this. Unfortunately, however, the hypocrisy of some, who even get to comedy excuses, are stumbling up and stumbling up, so that the desired decision is not taken. I say'responsible', because for decades the Greek Immigration Organizations have submitted this request and on the one hand the Greek permanent residents of the country are in full agreement with this facility. Ladies and gentlemen, the seasons we have been experiencing and the trials that our country has been experiencing over recent years, I believe that they have made clear to us all the need for the Greek adversity to be united, for the bonds to be strengthened, for the forces and the potential that have been proved throughout the globe, so that the Greeks can contribute as excellent ambassadors to the strong presence of our country in the global events and the upward evolution of its course. Of course this presupposes taking part in the country's politically hypothetical decisions, i.e. rights along with obligations. In order to achieve this, it is a condition to facilitate voting from the place where they are resident. My group, ladies and gentlemen, is aware of this need and of this need for foreign compatriots over time. I recall the effort by the governments of Kostas Karamanlis, Antonis Samaras, and today the clear and decisive attitude of Mr Mitsotakis, who has consistently taken the initiative in his election promises to hold a serious dialogue with all the parties, the organizations of relatives and all the parties involved. In fact, although we believe that Greeks abroad should be allowed to exercise their electoral right, without conditions and conditions, without any discrimination and exceptions, by way of written vote, an effective and secret method used by all the countries, the need arising from Article 51(4) of the Constitution for the approval by two hundred Members of Parliament for the adoption of such a law, has forced the Government to negotiate and compromise in the interests of finding the common component and the maximum consensus. I say in summary - because it is worthwhile - that the debates in the cross-party have resulted in five points, which I shall not mention, for a time economy. I will only say this, which concerns today's debate, that there should be an amendment to Article 54 of the Constitution in order to remove any doubt that the introduction of restrictions on the facilitation of foreign voters is a constitutional solution. Ladies and gentlemen, today we are called upon to take the first step, to consult ourselves for the first time on an issue relating to the credibility of the political system towards the Greeks, inside and outside the country. Any other position, ladies and gentlemen of counter-population, will mean evasion and hypocrisy. The opinion of some of us in this House is probably not of interest to you, but you should be interested in the opinion of the people who are being watched and who are, on their behalf, all in this Chamber. You know, decisions in sin are understood by everyone, as everyone sees behind words and understands that a possible refusal of you to add Article 54 will come from your fear that the vote of the Greeks abroad will not move in your interest. And why would that come to a conclusion? Because foreign voters are people of work, of creation, of production, of risk, because this shows that they left the confidence of paternity restaurants and left to work. They are Greeks with love for the country, where nostalgia makes it even stronger and possibly this profile does not conform to the culture and philosophy of the Left. Ladies and gentlemen, I do not want your decision to lead to such conclusions. On the contrary, I would like to prove that we are responsible for national issues and for the sake of our country and citizens. I therefore call on you all to vote in favour of the addition of Article 54, to justify those who trusted us and to strengthen the links between the Greekism of the earth and the mother of Greece. Thank you.
